2

[Garcia, 2020] reviewed by H. Yildirim
The PhD thesis of Garcia [2020] focuses on advancing methods for predicting the
fatigue life of mild and high-strength steels, with a particular emphasis on gen-
eralised local approaches capable of handling diverse cases, including multiaxial
fatigue. The research identifies the most suitable stress-based criteria and devel-
ops a 3D framework extending an existing 2D strain-based approach. To achieve
this, extensive multiaxial fatigue tests are performed on welded specimens made
of S690QL high-strength steel (HSS) and S235JR mild steel. Advanced measure-
ment techniques, such as neutron and X-ray diffraction for residual stresses, weld
geometryscanning, andElectronBackscatterDiffraction(EBSD)formicrostructure
analysis, are employed. Additionally, Stereo Digital Image Correlation (DIC) and
Alternative Current Potential Drop (ACPD) methods are used to track different
stages of fatigue damage.
Severalstress-basedmultiaxialfatiguecriteriaareevaluatedusingtheeffectivenotch
stress approach on experimental data and literature databases. Statistical analyses
reveal that von Mises equivalent stress, Findley [1959], and Carpinteri and Spagnoli
[2001] criteria reliably predict fatigue life under proportional and non-proportional
loading, leading to safe estimations when combined with updated FAT categories.
Conversely, the principal stress criterion is found to be unsuitable for multiaxial
loading.
The thesis introduces a novel 3D generalised strain-based approach to estimate
fatigue damage stages, from initiation to short crack propagation and final fracture.
This method integrates an ”area method” to compute the fatigue effective stress
and derives an equivalent fatigue strain history using a validated continuum single
element model (CSEM). The approach accurately captures elastic-plastic material
behaviour and mitigates singularity effects near crack tips. Mean stress effects and
residual stresses are modelled, albeit with some simplifications.
Finally, the influence of key parameters governing fatigue life, including mean stress,
residual stress, surface integrity, and material anisotropy, is assessed through para-
metric analysis. Experimental validation, supported by DIC measurements, demon-
strates the approach’s accuracy in predicting initiation lives and crack plane orien-
tations. This work significantly enhances fatigue life estimation methods, providing
robust tools for practical engineering applications.
Garcia [2020] extracts the all relevant available data until 2020. They reproduce
typical SN curves according to various models, such as IIW, Eurocode, Findley
[1959], and Carpinteri and Spagnoli [2001] and, modified CS in the local approaches.
Below, as examples, two reference calculations are provided for EC3 (EN 1993-1-
9:2005 [Council of European Union, 2005]) and the IIW recommendation [Hob-
bacher and F., 2016] resulted with Figure 1. For the remaining calculation cases
presented in Figure 2, the reader is referred to the corresponding reference [Garcia,
2020].
The thesis uses the Eurocode3 standard (EN 1993-1-9:2005 [Council of European
Union, 2005]) and the IIW recommendation Hobbacher and F. [2016], in which both
are based on the Basquin’s equation as shown in Equation 1, and based on on stress
amplitudes.

The parameter n represents the number of test data points. As stated by Hobbacher
andF.[2016], ifthenumberofdatapointsisn < 10orifthedataarenotsufficiently
evenly distributed to determine m accurately, a fixed value of m should be adopted
from other comparable tests. For instance, a value of m = 3 is recommended for
uniaxial normal tests, while m = 5 is suggested for shear tests in the case of steel.
To verify whether the pre-fixed slope m remains reasonable, the standard deviation
of m should be determined. In other scenarios, direct fitting of the data is also
| considered | an appropriate |     | approach. |     |     |     |     |
| ---------- | -------------- | --- | --------- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
The IIW’s general procedure for determining S-N curves is based on estimating con-
fidence limits on the prediction limits, known as tolerance limits. This methodology
provides more conservative S-N curves, accounting for uncertainties in population
statistics derived from small sample sizes [Schneider and Maddox, 2003].
The characteristic values x of the S-N curves at a confidence level of α = 95%
k
and their upper bound at a survival probability of α = 5% (corresponding to 5%
and 95% probability of failure, respectively) are computed from the statistical mean
value of log∆σ = x¯. These values are determined based on a two-sided tolerance
limit with a 75% confidence level for the mean x¯, as expressed in the following
equation:

The statistical factor k serves as a crucial parameter for evaluating a set of fatigue
data. As outlined in the IIW guidelines [Hobbacher and F., 2016], this factor ac-
counts for the variance within the dataset, the probability distributions of both the
mean and variance values within their respective confidence intervals, and the dis-
tinction between the distribution of the complete dataset and that of a sample (i.e.,
| Gaussian | versus t-distribution). |     |     |     |     |
| -------- | ----------------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

theupperboundoftheconfidenceintervalforthevariance. Itsgeneralmathematical
| formulation | is given | as follows: |     |     |     |

| IIW    | and EC3    | interaction | equations | approaches |     |
The interaction equations proposed by IIW and EC3 are utilized to character-
ize the test results, as illustrated in Figure 1 and tables on page 10. Initially,
the characteristic curves suggested by IIW [Hobbacher and F., 2016] and Sonsino
[2009], namely FAT225/3 and FAT160/5, are considered. The findings indicate that
both approaches result in non-conservative estimates, with the characteristic curves
FAT155/3 and FAT161/3 derived from IIW and EC3 being significantly lower than
| the FAT225/3 | curve. |     |     |     |     |
| ------------ | ------ | --- | --- | --- | --- |
Recent models from the literature are sometimes complex in their application for
practitioners, and rather simpler solutions are suggested in the Eurocode 3 (EC3)
| and the | more updated | IIW recommendations. |     |     |     |
| ------- | ------------ | -------------------- | --- | --- | --- |

|     |     |     |     |             |     | +         |             | ≤         | 1.0 |     |     | (20) |
|     |     |     |     | ∆σ          |     |           | ∆τ          |           |     |     |     |      |
|     |     |     |     |             | R,d |           | R,d         |           |     |     |     |      |
The assumption is made that normal and shear stresses contribute to the total
damage independently. As for the IIW, one has to note that the application of the
above criteria is not restricted to nominal or structural stress components, it is also
| valid for | local | stress | components. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --------- | ----- | ------ | ----------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
As for the IIW interaction equation, the EC3 Eq.20 can be rewritten in terms of
the local nominal stresses and using Eq. 7 as in the following Eq.21,

| of the    | R2 method, [Skibicki | and   | Karolczuk, | 2025].         |     |     |

| [Skibicki | and Karolczuk,       | 2025] | reviewed   | by A. Cernescu |     |     |
An interesting approach is proposed in the reviewed paper that uses an error tol-
erance concept to assess and quantify the life prediction errors associated with ap-
proximate methods for determining constant model parameters in multiaxial fatigue
life prediction models. Usually, a multiaxial fatigue prediction model can be math-
| ematically | expressed | in the | form of: |                 |     |      |
| ---------- | --------- | ------ | -------- | --------------- | --- | ---- |
|            |           |        | f(ε      | ,σ ,t,k ) = g(N | )   | (25) |
|            |           |        | ij       | ij i            | f   |      |
where the left side of the previous equation is the multiaxial fatigue model and the g
function is the uniaxial tension or torsion fatigue curve. As equation (25) shows, the
multiaxial fatigue prediction models use constant parameters which are dependent
| on  | the fatigue lives, | N . |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ------------------ | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
f
The paper introduces a method for approximating the constant parameter, k, by
R2
maximizing the coefficient of determination (R2 method), calculated from uni-
axial and torsional fatigue life curves. The process is shown in figure 3 and it is
used on seven well-established multiaxial fatigue models, using experimental data
| from | eight metallic | materials. |     |     |     |     |
| ---- | -------------- | ---------- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
In general, the parameter k in multiaxial fatigue models is defined as a function
of the ratio between the torsional and axial fatigue limits and is a unique mate-
rial parameter. In this paper, three methods for determining the parameter k are
described, one of which is proposed by the authors (R2 method).
1. Life-dependent model parameters, as LD method. This method assumes that
the parameter k is dependent on the number of cycles in the axial fatigue
curve, respectively the number of cycles in the torsional fatigue curve. For a
given stress state, σ , the parameter k is found which determines the number
ij
of cycles in axial fatigue, N and the number of cycles in torsion fatigue, N ,
|     |     |     | f,σ |     |     | f,τ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
figure 4.
2. Constant model parameters, as N0 method. For an arbitrarily chosen fatigue
life, N , the parameter k is a function of the torsional fatigue limit, respectively

the model parameters are found by maximizing the coefficient of determination

Using the three methods on different materials, multiaxial loading modes and
different multiaxial fatigue models, it is noted that the accuracy of estimating
the multiaxial fatigue life is increased in the case of determining the constant
parameter k by life-dependent methods (LD method and R2 method). The
authors recommend the use of life-dependent methods for estimating k param-
| eters in multiaxial | fatigue | models. |     |     |     |

| [Tveit | et al., 2024] | reviewed |     | by K. Freytag |     |     |     |     |     |
| ------ | ------------- | -------- | --- | ------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
This article describes a continuum approach to multiaxial high-cycle fatigue mod-
eling for ductile metallic materials. In their introduction, the authors present a
|     | concise definition | of  | the | problem: |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ------------------ | --- | --- | -------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
Considering cyclic multiaxial stress histories, the traditional concept of
HCF analysis combines three model components: A fatigue limit func-
tion, a cycle counting method, and a fatigue damage accumulation law.
The fatigue limit function defines the multiaxial endurance limit, separat-
ing safe from unsafe stress configurations while providing a measure of the
multiaxial stress that is comparable to experimental data which typically
governs uniaxial stress. Whether the fatigue limit function is formulated
based on resolved stresses on material planes or invariants of the stress
tensor, theinputtothetraditionalfatiguelimitfunctionsisgiveninterms
of cyclic mean stresses and stress amplitudes. To deal with the ambigu-
ities that arise when the special case of constant amplitude loading does
not apply, a cycle-counting method is employed. The rain-flow counting
technique is popularly used to reduce randomly varying continuous stress
histories into a finite number of discrete cycles, allowing the calculation
of cyclic mean stresses and stress amplitudes for input in the fatigue limit
function. Combined with a damage accumulation rule, the fatigue life
may be calculated through the summation of the damage associated with
|     | each | synthetic | cycle. |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ---- | --------- | ------ | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
The article reports on a new development within the class of Mises-based formula-
tions for the description and prediction of the fatigue limit under multiaxial loading
conditions. Theseaimathandlingarbitrarymultiaxialstresshistories,whichremain
a challenge for the traditional cycle counting-based multiaxial HCF models due to
their intrinsic presumption that any multiaxial stress history consists of distinct cy-
cles. TheauthorsstartfromtheworkofOttosenetal.[2008], inwhichanendurance
surface separates the stress space into two domains: a safe domain, where fatigue
damage cannot develop, and an unsafe domain, where fatigue damage can develop
under certain conditions. The Ottosen model uses an incremental formulation using
a deviatorically modified backstress and a damage evolution, which increase when
thestressstateislocatedoutsidetheendurancesurfaceandismovingawayfromthe
endurance surface. No cycles are counted when using this approach, instead it uses
a deviatoric effective stress measure σσσˆ that is a function of the backstress-modified
e

[Meggiolaro and de Castro, 2022] Review by Szymon Duda
This manuscript delivers the enhanced modeling technique, which covers the effect
of peak/mean stress on multiaxial fatigue life. The problem is detailed described
in the introduction as follows: Importance of the normal stress in fatigue dam-
age development is recognized especially in directional-damage materials. However,
available multiaxial models for crack initiation show a good accuracy mainly un-
der in-phase loading with insignificant magnitude of mean stresses. Considering
out-of-phase loading and/or effect of men stress only few models provide reliable re-
sults. The article reports on a development of Kujawski’s-based deviatoric damage
parameter for the prediction of tensile-driven cracks initiation. Authors compare
developed model with tensile-based critical plane models available in the literature
such as modified Smith-Watson-Topper (SWT), Kujawski and Ince Parameter. The
authors chose Kujawski parameter as it shows a good prediction even in case of
compression-compression loading (which is challenging due to the plastic buckling
and identification of cracks, which remaining closed). The adopted parameter is
named A90(T) (initiation of tensile-driven cracks, case A-plane oriented 90◦), and
it calculates deviatoric damage parameter not only on the principal planes, but also
20

in other planes over all A90 cross-section, it can be extended for variable amplitude
loading. The form of A90(T) model is presented below:

The calibration of the proposed damage parameter requires experimental data from
specimens in which tensile-driven cracks initiate. Therefore, uniaxial data is the
most suitable choice. The calibration of Coffin-Manson parameters involves project-
ing stress and strain loading histories. If variable amplitude loading is considered,
a simple uniaxial rainflow counting method can be applied. Subsequently, a dam-
age accumulation calculation should be performed, for example, using Miner’s rule.
This process must be conducted for each candidate plane to determine the direction
of the critical plane.
Using the experimental data, a comparison of the model performance for various
materials are shown in Figures 1 to 3 in the article.
[Ince and Glinka, 2014] reviewed by L. Pejkowski

low- and high-cycle fatigue ranges, mean stress effects, and loading paths. The pa-
rameterisformulatedintwovariants. Thefirstvariant,referredtoasthe”maximum
generalized strain energy”, is expressed as:

The authors highlighted that the key strengths of the parameter lie in its applica-
bility to both low- and high-cycle regimes, along with its ability to account for the
mean stress effect, loading path, and constitutive behavior. A notable advantage
is that the parameter does not require any material-related constants, making it
particularly convenient. However, its applicability under random loading conditions
was not discussed. The appropriate cycle counting method and the physical quan-
tity to which it should be applied remain unclear. Moreover, the issue of damage
|      | accumulation |       | was      | not addressed. |              |     |     |     |     |     |     |

is the normal strain, and γ is the shear strain acting on a specified plane. As an
example, a through-thickness sectional plane at the weld toe was provided. The

on the critical plane. The coefficient L accounts for the different contributions of
shear and normal components in the fatigue damage process. The critical plane is
identified as the one with the highest shear strain energy density among all planes
passing through the considered point. If multiple planes exhibit the same shear
strain energy density, the plane with the highest normal strain energy density is
selected. According to the authors, the parameter’s key advantages are its ability
to adjust the relative influence of shear and normal components and its inclusion of
|     | mean | shear | and | normal | stress | effects. |     |     |     |     |     |     |

The authors state that when η ≤ α′, fatigue cracking is primarily governed by the
maximum normal stress, making the maximum normal stress plane the critical one.
For α′ < η < β′, the critical plane is determined by the maximum damage criterion.

The paper contains an extended literature review highlighting lacks of some criteria
like Grubsic Simbu¨rger, Liu-Zenner or FKM guideline regarding the mean shear
stress effect. This effect is then examined using 25 different materials in a HAIGH
diagram showing an elliptical relationship that best fits the data. Thus, a new
criterion is invented based on the Marin-criterion, incorporating the mean stress
effects via the hydrostatic stress beside the mean value of the second invariant.
Through this, the observed ellipse in the HAIGH diagram can be represented. It is
derived as in equation (56) where the unity case D = 1 displays the strength limit.

The damage is assumed when the parameter D exceeds unity. Its calculation is
performed by finding the maximum over the values l,m,n which represent the direc-
tion of a vector and, thus, the rotation of the damage plane. The relation is based
on the assumption that both, shear and normal stresses, must be revised for the
evaluation of damage-relevant loading. Subsequently, the normal stress σ en-
n,max

τ is handled as driving force for the creation of extrusions and intrusions. From

For the validation of the damage parameter, the authors used a often cited set of ex-
perimental data from Nishihara and Kawamoto and Susmel and Petrone containing
different types of materials (steel, cast iron, aluminium). The according material
data was not given in this paper, but in the subsequent paper [Ninic and Stark,
2007]. No mean stress effect can be derived from this data and also the underlying
load cases cannot be assigned to certain tabular value and errors in the paper as
only values of the stress tensor are given. Based on the data, the criterion reaches a
mean error of 0.672% with standard deviation of 6.43% what is slightly better than
the criteria of McDiarmid as well as Carpinteri and Spagnoli which were evaluated
in parallel.

For the validation of the damage parameter, the authors used a larger set of dif-
ferent types of materials such as steel with different compositions, cast iron and
aluminium under several loading cases. These incorporate primarily reversing tests
with in- and out-of-phase loading combining bending and torsion and two cases with
included mean stress for torsion and bending, respectively. With these load cases,
the criterion reaches an average confidence interval between -7.57% and 6.37% and,
thus, slightly outperforms the criteria of McDiarmid, Carpinteri and Spagnoli, El-
| lipse quadrant | and | Ellipse Arc | which were | tested in parallel. |     |

| [Mamiya |     | et  | al., | 2011] | reviewed | by K. | Freytag |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| ------- | --- | --- | ---- | ----- | -------- | ----- | ------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
A multi-axial model for fatigue life estimation is formulated in terms of a piecewise
ruled S–N surface. The first ruled surface considers the sum of a deviatoric stress
amplitude and the maximum hydrostatic stress as an exponential function of the
number of cycles to failure. For small magnitudes of the hydrostatic stress, another
surface is defined by considering only the effect of the deviatoric stress amplitude
upon the expected fatigue life. The deviatoric stress amplitude was computed by
|     | the | maxi- | mum | prismatic |     | hull concept. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | ----- | --- | --------- | --- | ------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| [Khalij |     | et al., | 2017] | reviewed |     | by H. | Yildirim |     |     |
| ------- | --- | ------- | ----- | -------- | --- | ----- | -------- | --- | --- |
The paper by Khalij et al. [2017] presents an improved multiaxial high-cycle fatigue
model based on the Gough and Nishihara & Kawamoto approaches. The work
addresses the critical need for accurate fatigue life prediction across a wide range of
metallic materials, from ductile to extremely brittle metals. The authors propose a
novel criterion that combines the equivalent shear stress amplitude, obtained from
a prismatic convex hull method, with the amplitude of the first stress invariant.
The paper makes several significant contributions to the field of multiaxial fatigue
analysis:
Improved Fatigue Criterion: The authors develop a new criterion that general-
izes the Gough and Nishihara & Kawamoto criteria to plane stress states while
|     |     | including |     | hydrostatic |     | stress | effects. |     |     |
| --- | --- | --------- | --- | ----------- | --- | ------ | -------- | --- | --- |
Prismatic Hull Method: The paper introduces the use of the ‘Prismatic Hull in
the Principal coordinate System (PHPS)’ method to measure equivalent shear
stress amplitude, which is shown to be effective for both proportional and
|     |     | non-proportional |     |     | loading | conditions. |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | ------- | ----------- | --- | --- | --- |
Material Range Extension: The proposed criterion is validated for a wide range
of materials, with particular attention to brittle metals where traditional cri-
|     |     | teria | often | fail. |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | ----- | ----- | ----- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
Phase Shift Consideration: The model properly accounts for the phase shift be-
tween normal stress components, which is shown to significantly affect fatigue
strength.
|     | The | authors | employ |     | both | analytical | and numerical | approach: |     |
| --- | --- | ------- | ------ | --- | ---- | ---------- | ------------- | --------- | --- |
• The equivalent shear stress amplitude is derived using the PHPS method in a
|     |     | 5-dimensional |     | deviatoric |     | stress | space. |     |     |
| --- | --- | ------------- | --- | ---------- | --- | ------ | ------ | --- | --- |
•
Aweightedcombinationoftheequivalentshearstressamplitudeandfirststress
|     |     | invariant |     | amplitude | forms | the | basis of the | new criterion. |     |
| --- | --- | --------- | --- | --------- | ----- | --- | ------------ | -------------- | --- |
•
The weight function α(r) is carefully derived to maintain isotropic behavior.
•
Extensive numerical validation is performed using the Generalized Reduced
|     |     | Gradient |          | (GRG) | algorithm.   |     |               |          |     |
| --- | --- | -------- | -------- | ----- | ------------ | --- | ------------- | -------- | --- |
|     | The | key      | equation | of    | the proposed |     | criterion is: |          |     |
|     |     | (cid:34) |          |       |              |     |               | (cid:35) |     |

| --- | ---- | --- | --- | --- | --- | ----- | -------- | --------------- | ------ |

31

The authors validate their model against extensive experimental data from various
sources:
• The criterion shows excellent agreement with bending-torsion tests across ma-
|     |     | terial | types | (Figure |     | 3 in the | article). |     |
| --- | --- | ------ | ----- | ------- | --- | -------- | --------- | --- |
• For biaxial tests, the model transitions smoothly from von Mises-like behavior
for ductile materials to Rankine-like behavior for brittle materials (Figures 4-5
|     |     | in  | the article). |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | ------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
•
The phase shift effects are properly captured, showing detrimental effects for
mild steels and beneficial effects for brittle materials (Figures 7, 10-12 in the
article).
• Themodelremainsconservativewhileimprovingaccuracycomparedtoexisting
criteria.
|     | The | paper | has | several | notable | strengths: |     |     |
| --- | --- | ----- | --- | ------- | ------- | ---------- | --- | --- |
Comprehensive Validation: The model is tested against a wide range of mate-
|     |     | rials | and | loading | conditions. |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | ----- | --- | ------- | ----------- | --- | --- | --- |
Theoretical Rigor: The mathematical derivation is sound and well-justified.
Practical Relevance: The criterion maintains simplicity while improving accu-
racy.
Phase Shift Handling: Proper treatment of phase effects addresses a known lim-
|     |     | itation |     | in many | existing | criteria. |     |     |
| --- | --- | ------- | --- | ------- | -------- | --------- | --- | --- |
While the paper makes significant advances, some limitations exist:
•
The model assumes sinusoidal loading, which may limit applicability to more
|     |        | complex      |           | loading | spectra.  |          |               |                  |
| --- | ------ | ------------ | --------- | ------- | --------- | -------- | ------------- | ---------------- |
|     |        | • Validation |           | is      | primarily | limited  | to high-cycle | fatigue regimes. |
|     |        | • The        | treatment |         | of mean   | stresses | could be      | further refined. |
|     | Future |              | work      | could   | explore:  |          |               |                  |
•
|     |     | Extension |     | to  | variable | amplitude | loading |     |
| --- | --- | --------- | --- | --- | -------- | --------- | ------- | --- |
•
|     |     | Application |     | to  | very | high-cycle | fatigue (VHCF) | regimes |
| --- | --- | ----------- | --- | --- | ---- | ---------- | -------------- | ------- |
•
|     |     | Incorporation |     |     | of microstructural |     | considerations |     |
| --- | --- | ------------- | --- | --- | ------------------ | --- | -------------- | --- |
Khalij et al. present a significant advancement in multiaxial fatigue criteria that
successfully bridges the gap between ductile and brittle material behavior. The pro-
posed criterion demonstrates improved accuracy across a wide range of materials
and loading conditions while maintaining computational efficiency. The work pro-
vides both theoretical insights and practical tools for fatigue analysis in engineering
applications.
| [Li | et  | al., 2015] |     | reviewed | by  | H. Yildirim |     |     |
| --- | --- | ---------- | --- | -------- | --- | ----------- | --- | --- |
The paper by Li et al. [2015] presents an innovative approach to multiaxial fatigue
life prediction that addresses limitations in conventional methods. The authors
focus on two often-neglected factors: stress amplitude ratio and stress level effects,
32

proposing a novel methodology based on equal-life curves, a new path-dependent
| factor, |       | and   | material | sensitivity |             | parameters.    |     |     |     |     |     |
| ------- | ----- | ----- | -------- | ----------- | ----------- | -------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The     | study | makes | several  |             | significant | contributions: |     |     |     |     |     |
• Critical analysis of four conventional multiaxial fatigue criteria (Von-Mises,
|     | Gough, |     | Matake, | and | Papadopoulos) |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ------ | --- | ------- | --- | ------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
•
Development of a multiaxial fatigue life surface concept based on equal-life
curves
•
Introduction of a new path-dependent factor (F ) for non-proportional loading
h
• Proposal of a material sensitivity parameter (M) to account for material-
|     | specific |     | responses |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | -------- | --- | --------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
• Comprehensivevalidationusingexperimentaldatafromfourdifferentmaterials
Theauthorsemployathree-partmethodology: Theapproachbeginsbyconstructing
| a   | multiaxial |     | fatigue | life surface |     | using: |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ---------- | --- | ------- | ------------ | --- | ------ | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
•
|     | Basic | S-N | curves | from | uniaxial | and | pure | torsion | tests |     |     |
| --- | ----- | --- | ------ | ---- | -------- | --- | ---- | ------- | ----- | --- | --- |
•
One representative proportional loading path (with λ ≈ τ /σ )
−1 −1
|     | • Exponential |     | fitting | of  | equal-life | curves: |       |      |        |     |      |
| --- | ------------- | --- | ------- | --- | ---------- | ------- | ----- | ---- | ------ | --- | ---- |

|     |               |     |         |     | Nj         | Nj      |       | Nj   | Nj     |     |      |
For non-proportional loading, the authors introduce a path-dependent factor:

A non-proportional correction factor is defined as a material sensitivity parameter:
|       |         |      |            |       |          | G     | = 1+F        | M          |        |     | (72) |

| The   | authors |      | validate   | their | approach | using | four         | materials: |        |     |      |
•
|     | LY12CZ     |     | aluminum | alloy |       |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ---------- | --- | -------- | ----- | ----- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     | • AlCu4Mg1 |     | aluminum |       | alloy |     |     |     |     |     |     |
|     | • SAE1045  |     | steel    |       |       |     |     |     |     |     |     |
|     | • AISI4140 |     | steel    |       |       |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| Key | results    |     | include: |       |       |     |     |     |     |     |     |
•
|     | Majority |     | of predictions |     | within | factor | of  | 3 of experimental |     | data |     |
| --- | -------- | --- | -------------- | --- | ------ | ------ | --- | ----------------- | --- | ---- | --- |
•
|     | Improved |     | accuracy | compared |     | to  | conventional |     | methods |     |     |
| --- | -------- | --- | -------- | -------- | --- | --- | ------------ | --- | ------- | --- | --- |
• Effective handling of both proportional and non-proportional loading
| Strengths |     | of  | the proposed |     | model: |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --------- | --- | --- | ------------ | --- | ------ | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
33

• Comprehensive consideration of stress amplitude ratio and stress level effects
• Novel path-dependent factor for non-proportional loading
• Material-specific sensitivity parameter
• Extensive validation with multiple materials
• Good agreement with experimental data
Limitations are:
• Requires additional proportional loading tests for model calibration
• Limited validation for complex loading histories
• Material parameter M requires circular path test data
• Primarily validated for high-cycle fatigue regime
The authors provide a thorough comparison with four conventional approaches:
Table 11: Comparison of multiaxial fatigue criteria
Criterion Advantages Limitations
Von-Mises Simple formulation Poor accuracy for non-proportional loading
Gough Good for ductile/brittle metals Limited to proportional loading
Matake Critical plane approach Multiple critical planes possible
Papadopoulos Mesoscopic scale basis Complex implementation
Li et al. present a valuable contribution to multiaxial fatigue analysis with their
equal-life curve based approach. The method effectively addresses limitations in
conventional criteria by incorporating stress amplitude ratio, stress level effects,
and material sensitivity to non-proportional loading. The comprehensive validation
demonstrates good predictive capability across multiple materials and loading con-
ditions. Future work could extend the approach to more complex loading histories
and additional material systems.
[Mei and Dong, 2016] reviewed by H. Yildirim
The article by Mei and Dong [2016] presents a new path-dependent fatigue damage
model for non-proportional multi-axial loading. The model is formulated based on
the Moment of Load Path (MLP) concept, which integrates strain energy densi-
ties contributed by normal and shear stresses, each weighted by a path-dependent
function. The model is validated using experimental data from welded joints un-
der stress-controlled conditions (HCF) and non-welded components under strain-
controlled conditions (LCF).
The model operates in two variable spaces:
√
• Stress plane: σ − βτ (for HCF)
√
• Strain plane: ε− βeγ (for LCF)
Themodelissemi-empirical, combiningphysicalprinciples(strainenergydensity)
with empirical calibration (material sensitivity parameters).
34

The model addresses non-proportional loading, where stress components vary
independently or with phase shifts.
Themodelhandlesvariable amplitudeloading, includingasynchronoussinusoidal
loading (e.g., bending and torsion with different frequencies).
The model uses the Path-Dependent Maximum Range (PDMR) method
for cycle counting, which identifies maximum stress/strain ranges and tracks path
lengths.
The MLP-based fatigue damage parameter is defined as:
∆σ = ∆σ (1+α·g )
NP e NP
where:
• ∆σ : Effective stress range for a proportional path.
e
• α: Material sensitivity to non-proportionality.
• g : Non-proportionality factor, calculated as:
NP
(cid:82)
D |p(σ,τ)dσ +q(σ,τ)dγ|
g = NP = AB
NP (cid:82)
D |p(σ,τ)dσ +q(σ,τ)dγ|
Max AB
Key parameters include:
• α: Material sensitivity to non-proportionality (determined experimentally).
• β,βe: Fatigue equivalency factors between tensile and shear stress/strain.
• p(σ,τ),q(σ,τ): Path-dependent weight functions.
The model is validated using two datasets:
Welded joints (HCF): Data from Sonsino and Kueppers [2001] and Yousefi et al.
[2001] under combined bending and torsion, including synchronous and asyn-
chronous loading.
Non-welded specimens (LCF): Data from Itoh et al. [1995] and Socie [1997]
under various strain-controlled load paths (e.g., ”diamond,” ”cross”).
Key findings from validation:
• The MLP-based parameter correlates well with experimental data, reducing
scatter compared to nominal stress/strain methods.
• The model handles complex load paths (e.g., asynchronous loading) effectively.
• The non-proportionality factor g successfully differentiates between propor-
NP
tional and non-proportional damage.
Main Conclusions:
• TheMLP-basedmodelprovidesaunifiedframeworkfornon-proportionalmulti-
axial fatigue, applicable to both HCF and LCF regimes.
• The model integrates cycle counting (PDMR) and damage parameter formu-
lation, addressing path-dependent effects.
35

• Validation shows excellent correlation with experimental data, including cases
where traditional methods (e.g., critical plane models) fail.
• Themodeliscomputationallyefficientandsuitableforvariableamplitudeload-
ing.
Range of Applicability:
• Materials: Ductile metals (e.g., steels, aluminum alloys).
• Loading: Non-proportional, variable amplitude, including asynchronous load-
ing.
• Regimes: High-cycle fatigue (HCF) and low-cycle fatigue (LCF).
[Pedersen, 2016] reviewed by H. Yildirim
The paper by Pedersen [2016] presents a comprehensive evaluation of multiaxial fa-
tigue criteria for welded joints using the notch stress approach. The study addresses
a critical gap in welding fatigue analysis by systematically comparing eight differ-
ent multiaxial criteria against a large experimental database (351 specimens). The
work is particularly valuable for engineering applications where welded structures
experience complex loading conditions.
The paper makes several significant contributions:
Systematic Comparison: First large-scale comparison of multiaxial fatigue cri-
teria for welded joints using notch stress approach
Safety Evaluation: Introducesquantitativesafetyassessmentthroughprobability
of non-conservative predictions
Experimental Database: Compiles and analyzes 351 test results from 12 inde-
pendent studies
Practical Recommendations: Provides clear guidance for proportional vs. non-
proportional loading cases
The study employs a rigorous methodology:
Notch Stress Analysis: Detailed FE modeling with r = 1 mm following IIW
ref
recommendations
Criteria Evaluation: Tests eight approaches:
• Equivalent stress (Principal, von Mises)
• Interaction equations (Eurocode 3, IIW)
• Critical plane (EESH, Carpinteri-Spagnoli, Findley, MWCM)
Safety Metric: Introduces prediction ratio p and calculates probability of non-
conservative predictions (P )
NC
Validation: Uses FAT225/3 and FAT160/5 SN curves as reference
The key safety evaluation metric is defined as:
∆σ
eq,N,i
p = (73)
∆σ
R,N
where p < 1 indicates non-conservative predictions.

The developed model, based on the characteristic plane, shows a large applicability
for various metallic materials (loading conditions, failure mechanisms). The model
is determined by minimizing the damage introduced by the amplitude of hydrostatic
stress that covers the mean stress effect.

Conclusions The method separates out-of-phase damage, unifies various NP load
casesthroughasinglescalarF , linksdirectlytostandardcountingtechniques,and
OP
has been validated against constant- and variable-amplitude NP tests on welded-
magnesium specimens, showing good agreement with experimental results.
Original experimental data [Bolchoun et al., 2017] The authors fabricated
tubular magnesium weld specimens (AZ31 and AZ61 alloys) by Nd:YAG laser weld-
ing of thin-walled tubes in an overlap configuration. The geometry, finite element
model and material properties are presented in Figure 9 and Tables 13–15. Servo-
hydraulic testing with two actuators enabled independent axial and torsional loads.
Wo¨hler-type (constant amplitude) and Gaßner-type (variable amplitude, 5 × 104
cycles in each test block) fatigue tests were performed in pure axial, pure torsional,
in-phase combined, and out-of-phase combined modes (Figure 10). A pronounced
fatigue life reduction was observed for out-of-phase loadings relative to in-phase.
41

Golos et al. [2014] propose a stress-based criterion for the high-cycle endurance limit
of metals under in-phase loading. The model maintains the geometric simplicity of
the Gough and Pollard ellipse while permitting any ratio of fully-reversed axial to
torsional fatigue limits. The approach needs: (i) the fully-reversed normal and shear
fatigue limits.

Conclusions Only the basic fatigue-limits σ and τ are needed, so no additional
−1 −1
parameter fitting is required. Because the formulation is purely stress-based, it is
most appropriate for nominally elastic, high-cycle loading that is in-phase; non-
proportional effects remain outside the scope of the paper. The authors validated
the criterion on six different steels and obtained a generally good correlation, even
though the alternative models given in Equations (89) and (90) occasionally showed
a slightly better fit. Furthermore, since the equation (93) matches the familiar limit
curves for conventional values of α, the criterion can be incorporated directly into
existing design codes that already employ Gough-type relations.

Conclusions The authors introduce a mean-shear–corrected, critical-plane model
that extends classical shear-based life criteria to multiaxial high-cycle fatigue with
non-zero mean stresses. By weighting instantaneous shear orientations they reduce
the search for the damaging plane to two orthogonal candidates, and by scaling the
shear amplitude with material ratios k and k they maintain a closed analytical
τ σ
form. Validation on thin-walled 7075-T651 aluminium tubes subjected to propor-
tional and non-proportional tension–torsion shows life predictions within a factor of
about three over the range 105–107 cycles, confirming the model’s soundness. The
approach therefore provides an economical yet physically grounded option for engi-
neering assessments whenever accurate axial and torsional S–N data are available
and low-cycle plasticity or pronounced load-sequence effects are not dominant.
Original experimental data [Wang et al., 2018] The study investigated high-
cycle fatigue behaviour of thin-walled tubular specimens made from 7075-T651 alu-
minium alloy under uniaxial, pure torsion and combined tension-torsion loading,

[Faes et al., 2024] reviewed by K. Hectors
The paper introduces a novel, rapid algorithm to quickly and reliably determine
the critical plane in large finite element (FE) models subjected to complex (mul-
tiaxial) fatigue loading. Originating from early fatigue criteria, the critical plane
is the material plane where fatigue damage is most likely to initiate (often defined
by maximum shear stress range). For multiaxial loading, the selection isn’t obvious
because the stress state is three-dimensional and time-varying. Traditional methods
require iterating over all possible plane orientations and are computationally very
expensive (especially when dealing with thousands of nodes in an industrial com-
ponent). The authors propose an optimization technique based on the Maximum
Variance Method (MVM) that dramatically reduces the computational effort. The
method transforms the search problem into finding the maximum variance of the
shear stress. This variance is a proxy for the shear stress range but is easier to
optimize because it eliminates explicit time dependence.
The proposed MVM-based search algorithm is based on a three-module structure.
Firstaglobalcoarsesearchoverthespaceofpossibleplaneorientationsisperformed.
The authors tested different numbers of starting guesses and found that using nine
uniformlydistributedinitialplanesprovidedanexcellentbalancebetweenspeedand
accuracy. Next a local search is performed once the initial guesses are set. Each
candidate is refined using a gradient-based optimizer. Here, the Sequential Least-
Squares Programming (SLSQP) algorithm was selected because it was fast and
robust compared to other methods. Finaly in the Pick Module, after refining the
solutions, the best candidate is chosen based on the highest shear stress amplitude.
The reciprocal solution (obtained by a 90◦ rotation) is also computed, and the one
with the higher associated normal stress is selected as the final critical plane.
The procedure is shown in the figure below. Starting from the stress history, the
covariance matrix of the stress tensor is determined. the global search module
selects a set of initial planes defined by the angles {θ,ϕ}, and computes the optimal
direction for each. The angle defines the direction along which the variance of
the shear stress is maximal on the considered planes. Following this, the resulting
angles triads {θ,ϕ} undergo refinement in the local search module via the SLSQP
algorithm. Theaimofthelocalsearchmoduleistorefineeachoftheinitialsolutions
towards the nearest local maximum, preferably as accurately and as efficiently as
possible. Transitioning to the final pick module, the solution with the highest shear
stress amplitude is selected. Its reciprocal counterpart is obtained by a 90◦ rotation
aroundthesymmetryaxis, andthemaximumnormalstressdeterminesthepreferred
choice between them.
The model is stress-based, relying solely on the time history of the stress tensor
to evaluate the shear stress range on arbitrary planes in the material. The main
objective is to determine the orientation of the plane , defined by its normal vector
and an in-plane direction, on which the range of resolved shear stress is maximized.
This shear stress range is computed over a full loading cycle using the expression
The method is semi-empirical, it is grounded in physics-based critical plane prin-
ciples but does not introduce new fatigue life equations or damage parameters.
Instead, it provides a fast and accurate numerical framework to identify the critical
plane for any such model. The analysis targets non-proportional, multiaxial loading
under constant amplitude (periodic) loading cycles.
51

Figure 13: Flowchart of the newly proposed optimization procedure
The authors performed a systematic numerical analysis (with over 10,000 loading
cases) to compare the performance of their method with existing algorithms. the
classic Gradient-Ascent-based optimization scheme, and to a gradient-less alterna-
tive that makes use of the closed-form solution for on a given plane. Their method
achieved a processing speed of about 10,000 nodes per CPU minute and demon-
strated very few sub-optimal outcomes. They also illustrate the algorithm on an
industrial design problem (an additively manufactured Ti–6Al–4V bearing house)
where the entire surface (approximately 100k nodes) can be analyzed in under 75
| seconds    | on a multi-threaded | C++ implementation. |     |     |
| ---------- | ------------------- | ------------------- | --- | --- |
| [Kujawski, | 2021] reviewed      | by K. Hectors       |     |     |
This paper introduces and discusses a new interpretation and modification of the
Smith-Watson-Topper (SWT) function. The paper proposes interpreting the SWT
function in terms of the total damaging energy density. This energy density is
defined as the sum of the damaging parts of the strain energy density and comple-
mentary energy density, corresponding specifically to the first quadrant in defined
damaging {σ ,ϵ } axes. It is postulated that only the total energy density associ-

| ated | with the first quadrant | contributes | to fatigue | damage. |
For fully reversed loading, the damaging axes σ and ϵ overlap with the σ and ϵ

axes. For cyclic loading with a positive mean stress (σ ≥ 0), the proposed function

| Modification |     | for Negative | Mean | Stress |     |     |     |
The original SWT function is known to provide a nonconservative estimation for
cyclic loading with a negative mean stress (σ < 0) or a stress ratio R < −1. The
m
paper proposes a modification to address this issue. This modification is based on
experimental results showing limited relaxation of negative mean stresses during
cyclic loading. Specifically, typical stabilised hysteresis loops for tests at a strain
ratio R = −2 indicated a stabilised mean stress equal to 1/3 of the initial mean
ϵ
stress value. To account for effects associated with σ < 0, the proposed modifica-
m
tion augments the maximum stress (σ ) by 1/3 of the absolute value of the mean
max

The proposed mSWT function is compared with experimental data for ductile iron
| and 7075-T651 |     | Al alloy | reported | in  | litarture.. |     |     |
| ------------- | --- | -------- | -------- | --- | ----------- | --- | --- |
These datasets include uniaxial cyclic loading with various stress ratios (R) or strain
ratios (R ), covering both positive and negative mean stresses. The validation also
ϵ
includes two-step test results for 7075-T651 Al alloy involving initial compression-
| compression |     | loading followed |     | by zero-to-tension |     | loading. |     |
| ----------- | --- | ---------------- | --- | ------------------ | --- | -------- | --- |
The mSWT function, demonstrates a consistent correlation of mean stress effects
for both positive and negative mean stresses. For both ductile iron and 7075-T651
Al alloy, data points for R > −1 and R < −1 coalesce within a narrow scatter
band, often aligning well with the fully reversed loading data (R = -1). The mSWT
function successfully corrects the nonconservative estimation for R < −1 (σ < 0)
m
that is observed with the original SWT function. The modified function is shown
to be capable of quantifying fatigue damage induced by compression-compression
loading where the maximum stress is negative (σ < 0), based on the two-step
max
test results.

Concluding remarks The PbP criterion has the primary advantage to tackle
the challenging issue of defining fatigue cycles under complex multiaxial loading by
decoupling the loading path along fixed directions, whose orientation is uniquely
determined by the shape of the loading path itself. Validation on six metals (AISI
304, Inconel 718, Al 6082-T6, SM45C, 18G2A and S45C) under proportional and
non-proportional loading conditions shows 75–95 % of lives predicted within a factor
2. The method offers an efficient alternative when detailed critical-plane searches
|     | are | impractical. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |

Conclusions The criterion presented by Gon¸calves et al. [2005] defines multiax-
ial endurance through a linear combination of the amplitude of a five-dimensional
deviatoric vector and the maximum principal stress. Validation on four hard steels
under in-phase and out-of-phase sinusoidal loading produced an error within ±15%,
generally more conservative than Crossland, Papadopoulos and Deperrois/Freitas
models. Its principal limitation is the assumption of iso-frequency sinusoidal load
histories.

in metallic alloys under multiaxial, multiblock, proportional loadings in (very) high
cycle fatigue. The model, called (V)HCF-DAM, is based on two main components:
(a) a stress function to accommodate the adopted fatigue criterion and (b) a char-

scheme for the damage model is devised assuming the availability of a rich database

Material data The authors have not developed own experimental multi-axial
fatigue data. Reference is made to results of uniaxial fatigue tests.
Summary This paper deals with the computation of the amplitude of shear stress
in multiaxial, high-cycle fatigue. The authors propose a surface approach based on
the convex hull enclosing the deviatoric stress history as a measure of the shear
stress amplitude. A convex hull is the smallest region containing the deviatoric
|     | stress path | under | investigation. |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ----------- | ----- | -------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
The choice of focusing on the convex hull is motivated by the observation that not
all the points of the considered deviatoric stress path, may contribute to fatigue
damage. Only the shear stresses on the convex hull boundary are considered to
affect the lifetime of the component in the high cycle fatigue regime.
Computation of the convex hull is performed using the standard QuickHull algo-
rithm (http://www.qhull.org/), based on the work by Barber et al and implemented
in the MATLAB function convhulln. The hull is represented by its m vertices
P ,··· ,P . Next the Gram–Schmidt process is adopted to calculate the shear
|     | 1   | m   |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
stress amplitude. The N-dimensional orthogonal directions are determined along
which the maximum distances between two opposite vertices of the convex hull are
achieved.
(cid:113)
(cid:80)N
The shear stress amplitude s is calculated as s = 1 (dmax)2 with dmax the
|     |     |     |     | a   |     | a   | j=1 j | j   |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | ----- | --- |
2
maximum distances between vertices of the convex hull measured along N orthog-
|     | onal directions. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

for different steels (25CrMo4, 34Cr4, GGG60, 30NCD16, 39NiCrMo3, 25CrMo4)
and aluminium (Al7075-T651). Two-, three-, and five-dimensional loading paths
are evaluated in the paper. Experimental data were collected from literature (fully
reversed axial and torsional fatigue limits, axial-torsional tests with asynchronous
harmonicstresses, biaxialtestswithasynchronousharmonicstresses). Thefollowing

where α,β,γ,δ,k are material parameters and N is the number of cycles to failure.
f
High accuracy is achieved when compared with alternative methods from the litera-
ture, such as the minimum circumscribed hypersphere approach and the maximum
prismatic hull approach.
[Zhang and Yao, 2010] reviewed by H. Yildirim
The paper by Zhang and Yao [2010] presents an improved critical plane-based cri-
terion for multiaxial high-cycle fatigue (HCF) analysis. The work addresses several
limitations in existing criteria, particularly for materials with fatigue limit ratios
(f /t ) between 1.25 and 2. The authors propose a novel nonlinear combination
−1 −1
of shear and normal stress parameters on the critical plane, along with a modified
approach for mean stress effects.
The paper makes several important contributions:
• Development of an improved multiaxial HCF criterion based on critical plane
approach
• Proposal of a nonlinear combination of shear amplitude (C∗) and maximum
a
normal stress (N∗ ) on the critical plane
max
• Introduction of a modified Goodman-based approach for mean stress effects
• Validation across multiple materials with 1.25 < f /t < 2
−1 −1
• Comprehensive comparison with four established criteria (Crossland, Sines,
McDiarmid, Papadopoulos)
The authors employ a critical plane approach with several key methodological com-
ponents:
The critical plane is defined as the plane experiencing maximum shear stress range,
following McDiarmid’s approach:

Zhang and Yao present a valuable contribution to multiaxial HCF analysis with
their improved critical plane criterion. The nonlinear combination of shear and nor-
mal stress parameters, along with the modified Goodman approach for mean stress,
shows superior performance compared to established criteria for certain material
classes. The comprehensive experimental validation demonstrates the model’s ef-
fectiveness, particularly for materials with fatigue limit ratios between 1.25 and 2.
Future work could extend the approach to low-cycle fatigue and broader material
classes.

The multiaxial fatigue behavior of the Ni-based superalloy GH4169 has been the
main objective of the manuscript by We et al. The authors evaluated several fa-
tigue criteria such as equivalent strain, maximum shear strain, Kandil-Brown-Miller
(KBM), Fatemi-Socie (FS) and Wu-Hu-Song (WHS).). Experimental data for axial,
torsional, proportional and nonproportional under fully reverse sinusoidal (R = −1)
has been used in the assessment of the highlighted criteria. The investigated mate-
rialexhibitedcyclic hardening, whichwasfurther intensified under non-proportional
loadingconditions. Consequently, thefatiguefailurecriterionmustaccountforthese
effects. Maximum shear strain and equivalent strain criteria provided unsatisfactory
results in life predictions (out of the 3 scatter bands). On the other hand, the FS
and KBM parameters show good agreement within the scatter bands of 3. The
most satisfactory agreement in results has been obtained for WHS damage parame-
ter, which takes advantage of the FS and Smith-Watson-Topper (SWT) parameters,
thus covering the shear- and tensile-driven microcracks as given in equation (124).

The Varvani-Farahani [2000] model combines shear and normal energy ranges eval-
uated on the critical plane, defined as the plane corresponding to the Mohr circles
of maximum shear strain and stress recorded during the loading and unloading cy-
cles of a load history. The model does not require any empirical fitting factor, as
the normal and shear energies have been weighted by the tensile and shear fatigue

The criterion presented by Varvani-Farahani [2000] defines an energy-based critical
plane factor, accounting for mean stress and out-of-phase cyclic hardening without
the use of empirical weighting factors. The calibration requires the standard axi-
al/torsional fatigue properties of the materials. The criterion has been applied to
seven metallic alloys (steels and Ni-base super-alloys) under in-phase, linear and
non-linear out-of-phase load histories. The model predicted fatigue lives within
factors 1.5–2.5; the widest scatter was observed for 304 stainless steel.

Based on the conventional concept of a Critical Plane (CP), the Effective Critical
Plane (ECP) approach proposed by Chiocca and Frendo [2024] averages stress and
strain tensors over a small, material-dependent volume, thereby preserving the ten-
sorial nature of the stress-strain field while incorporating local stress-gradient effects
within a linear-elastic regime. The approach requires two fully-reversed uniaxial fa-
tigue curves, each generated from specimens with a different notch severity.
Step 1 – Identification of the critical node The process begins by locating
the critical node in the component (or the finite-element model) that is, the node
exhibiting the highest local CP factor, and computing the volume-averaged stress
σ¯ and strain ε¯ tensors over a process volume defined by the radius r , which is
c
initially treated as a calibration parameter. The averaged tensors can be evaluated

Step 3 – Control radius calibration and fatigue endurance curve Af-
ter selecting the calibration dataset, the optimal control radius is determined by
minimising the difference between the predicted and the experimentally measured
fatigue-life residuals. Because the calibration phase requires a very large number
of Critical-Plane factor evaluations, optimized strategies can be employed to speed
up the analysis, either general optimisation techniques valid for any load case and
material model [Chiocca et al., 2023, Sgamma et al., 2024], or dedicated short-
cuts that exploit proportional loading under a linear-elastic material assumption

Conclusions The ECP framework maintains the fracture-plane orientation of the
classical CP method (as long as the control radius remains small [Frendo et al.,
2025]), while the averaging tensors step provides a fast and reliable tool for fatigue
lifepredictionundercomplexloadingconditions. Thecriterionhasbeenvalidatedon
different notched geometries and loading conditions for a low carbon steel [Chiocca
and Frendo, 2024] and a ductile cast iron GJS-600 [Chiocca et al., 2025].

This paper describes a new multiaxial fatigue theory, improving prediction accuracy
by 21.1% over Lu criterion, validated on 394 samples across 13 materials and 37
|     | loading | conditions. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |

Thisarticlediscussesvariousformsandclassificationsoffatigueparameterfunctions
used in multiaxial fatigue assessment, such as equivalent summation and equivalent
product. Fatigue criteria are categorized based on concepts like critical plane (crit-
ical vs. non-critical), failure mode (normal vs. shear), material response (stress,
67

strain, energy), and fatigue life (high vs. low cycle). Modern multiaxial fatigue
models have evolved from uniaxial and equivalent models, with many criteria de-
veloped over time. The diversity arises from differences in critical plane direction,
materialresponsecomponentsanditems, constitutivebehavior(elastic/plastic), and
| cyclic factors | such as | range, amplitude, | and mean | values. |     |
| -------------- | ------- | ----------------- | -------- | ------- | --- |
2. Multiaxial fatigue space In the author’s opinion, multiaxial fatigue pre-
diction can be divided into two parts. One is the description of material response
status resulted from loading, the other one is the determination of material depen-
dent coefficients.
2.1. Definition of multiaxial fatigue space This section introduces three
parameters(P1,P2,P3)usedtocharacterizemultiaxialfatigue. P1(radiusofMohr
circle) represents the equivalent shear or distortion parameter, while P2 (center of
Mohr circle) indicates the equivalent normal or volumetric parameter. P3 reflects
thechangeinprincipaldirectionovertimeandrequiresareferenceformeasurement.
The concept of multiaxial fatigue space is explored, composed of fatigue basic units
that describe material response changes. Multiple ‘points’ or planes are necessary
to fully capture the material status, with methods unified through P1, P2, and P3.

2.2. Discussion of out-of-phase failure This section explores in-phase and

damage due to changing dangerous deformation directions. Relative distortion de-
formation effectively describes this behavior. Parameter P3 represents out-of-phase
effects, while P1 and P2 capture in-phase responses using mean and amplitude val-

| [Saintier | et  | al., 2013] |     | reviewed | by  | S. Duda |     |     |     |
| --------- | --- | ---------- | --- | -------- | --- | ------- | --- | --- | --- |
The proposed high-cycle multiaxial fatigue criterion demonstrated good predictive
capabilities under both proportional and non-proportional multiaxial variable am-
plitude loadings. It was successfully validated on various metallic materials such as
ER7, SAE1045, C35N, St35, and EN-GJS800-2, and applied to a full-scale compo-
nent–arailwaywheel. Akeyadvantageofthisapproachisitsnon-localformulation,
69

which inherently accounts for stress gradient effects without requiring prior defini-
tion of the gradient direction, making it well-suited for complex loading scenarios.
The developed energy-based damage parameter is derived from the strain energy
density and assumes macroscopic elastic shakedown. Since the focus is on the high-
cycle fatigue regime, macroscopic plastic strains are negligible, justifying this as-
sumption. Application of the method requires identification of material parameters
(β,σ∗)based on fatigue strengths under fully reversed loading (tension–compression,
rotating bending, and torsion) on unnotched specimens. Here, β describes the mate-
rial’s sensitivity to stress triaxiality, while σ∗ represents the fatigue threshold stress
amplitude. The developed method does not require any cycle counting technique
and has been implemented as a post-processor for finite element software enabling
its application in industrial settings. However, some limitations remain. The model
does not yet account for mean stress effects and requires further validation, espe-
cially on notched specimens and a wider range of materials. Future work should also
address the role of compressive mean stresses, which are currently not distinguished
in the formulation.

Mahadevan[2005]andamesoscopic-scalecriterionsuchasPapadopoulos[2001]were

The research introduces a novel fatigue damage model built upon two primary inno-
vations: (1) a computationally efficient and physically intuitive method for quanti-
fying the degree of loading non-proportionality (f′ ), and (2) a refined critical plane
np
fatigue damage parameter (FDP) that accounts for the detrimental effect of normal
stress without relying on ambiguous or component-specific fitting constants. The
proposed model is validated against extensive experimental data for three distinct
metallic materials (304 stainless steel, sintered porous iron, and CuZn37 brass) un-
der a wide variety of load paths. The results demonstrate that the new model offers
superior predictive accuracy and robustness compared to established frameworks,
such as the Fatemi-Socie (FS), Chen, and Itoh models, representing a significant
advancement in the practical application of multiaxial fatigue analysis.
Theproposedmultiaxialfatiguemodelconsistsoftwonovel,synergisticcomponents:
a generalized non-proportionality factor and a refined damage parameter.
1. A generalized non-proportionality factor (f′ ): A key innovation of the
np

of relying on complex geometric operations in multi-dimensional strain space, the
authors propose using the Pearson correlation coefficient, C(ϵ ,γ ), to measure the
n s

The proposed model was subjected to rigorous experimental validation using fatigue
data for three materials with diverse mechanical properties: Type 304 stainless steel
(ductile, high hardening), sintered porous iron, and CuZn37 brass. The validation
dataset was comprehensive, covering 14 distinct load paths that included uniaxial
tension, pure torsion, proportional biaxial loading, and a variety of complex NP
paths (e.g., 30◦, 45◦, 60◦, and 90◦ out-of-phase sinusoidal, square, and butterfly
paths).
The predictive accuracy of the proposed model was compared against three widely
recognizedmodels: Fatemi-Socie(FS),Chen, andItoh. Theanalysisshowedthatfor
all three materials, the predictions of the proposed model were consistently superior.
The vast majority of its life predictions fell within the standard factor-of-2 scatter
bands. In contrast, the FS, Chen, and Itoh models frequently produced predictions
outsidethefactor-of-3bands, particularlyforthemoreseverenon-proportionalload-
ing cases.
Inconclusion,bydevelopingageneralized,computationallysimplenon-proportionality
factorandarefineddamageparameterfreeofambiguousfittingconstants,themodel
achieves a rare combination of high accuracy, robustness, and physical interpretabil-
ity.
The primary implication for practicing engineers is the availability of a more reli-
able model for the design and analysis of components subjected to real-world service
loading. Because the model’s components are based on fundamental, cycle-based
physical quantities rather than empirical curve-fitting to specific load cases, it pro-
vides a strong foundation for future extension into other domains, such as variable
| amplitude | loading | or  | thermomechanical |     | fatigue. |     |     |     |

[Bufalari et al., 2025] reviewed by K. Hectors
The validation of advanced multiaxial fatigue assessment models is often hindered
by a scarcity of high-fidelity experimental data, particularly for welded joints under
complex, non-proportional loading conditions. Existing fatigue design standards
and their associated S-N curves are typically derived from tests on reference-quality
welds. For modern, high-quality welded joints, such as those used in demanding
maritime applications, these standards may be overly conservative, leading to inef-
ficient and over-designed structures.
This study addresses this knowledge gap by pursuing three primary objectives.
First, it utilizes a unique, high-capacity six-degree-of-freedom (6-DoF) hexapod
test rig to generate novel mode-{I, III} multiaxial fatigue data for high-quality
steel welds. Second, it evaluates the performance of two leading fatigue assessment
criteria: the Effective Notch Stress (ENS, S ) and the Total Stress (S ), against
e T
this new dataset. Finally, it investigates the influence of specific quality attributes
(e.g., residual stress, weld geometry, material microstructure) on fatigue resistance
to determine if a dedicated S-N resistance curve for high-quality welds is justified
and necessary for accurate lifetime prediction. The research moves beyond simply
confirming that higher quality yields better performance. It seeks to quantify pre-
cisely how the fatigue damage mechanism itself is altered, a critical distinction for
advanced engineering design.
The experimental campaign was conducted using a custom-built, hydraulically con-
trolled hexapod (Stewart platform). Unlike simulators designed for large motions,
thisrigisoptimizedformechanicaltesting, deliveringlargeloads(e.g., axialforceup
to ±1000 kN, bending moment up to ±400 kNm) with high precision and stability.
Its 6-DoF architecture permits independent control over the amplitude, frequency,
andphaseofallthreeforcesandthreemoments, enablingthesimulationofcomplex,
non-proportional service loading conditions that are otherwise difficult to replicate
experimentally.
Thestudyusedtubular, double-sidedT-jointspecimensmachinedfromasinglesolid
bar of S355J2G3+N grade steel. This design ensures that fatigue failure initiates
and propagates from the weld toe, avoiding confounding failures at other geometric
features. The welds were produced using a certified, robotized process to meet
stringent offshore quality standards (Visual Class B, Magnetic Particle Level 2X).
The ”high quality” of these welds was quantitatively verified through extensive
metallurgical analysis, which revealed several key differences compared to reference-
quality welds:
• Homogeneous microstructure: the welds exhibited a more gradual mi-
crostructuraltransitionbetweenthebasemetal(BM),heat-affectedzone(HAZ),
and fusion zone (FZ).
• Lower hardness: Vickers hardness maps showed significantly lower peak
hardnessinthe HAZ(343HV2vs. 413HV2 inreferencewelds)andasmoother
hardness gradient. This indicates a lower cooling rate during welding, a condi-
tion associated with reduced tensile residual stresses and fewer/smaller initial
defects.
• Favorable geometry: the welds possessed a large and consistent weld toe
74

notch radius (ρ ≈ 3.12 mm), which serves to reduce the local stress concentra-
tion factor.
A comprehensive test matrix of 72 specimens was executed. The program included
uniaxial (mode-I bending and mode-III torsion) and combined mode-{I, III} mul-
tiaxial loading. The conditions covered constant amplitude (CA) and variable am-
plitude (VA) spectra, as well as proportional (P, in-phase) and non-proportional
(NP, 90◦ out-of-phase) loading. To isolate the effects of residual stress, tests were
performed on specimens in both as-welded (AW) and thermally stress-relieved (SR)
conditions.
Two advanced stress-based models were used to analyze the fatigue data:
• Effective Notch Stress (S ): an intact-geometry parameter that character-
e
izes fatigue damage by averaging the through-thickness notch stress distribu-
tion over a material characteristic length, ρ∗. This approach models fatigue as
a process governed by the stress state in the material volume near the notch.
• Total Stress (S ): a cracked-geometry parameter derived from fracture me-
T
chanics. This model calculates fatigue life by integrating a crack growth law
from a presumed initial defect size (a ) to a final failure size (a ). It explicitly
i f
accounts for the stress intensity at a growing crack tip, incorporating notch
effects and local elastoplasticity through a coefficient, n.
The central finding of the study is that the high-quality welds exhibit a fatigue
behaviorfundamentallydifferentfromthatofreference-qualitywelds. Whenplotted
on a log-log scale, the S-N data for the hexapod specimens show a significantly
steeper slope (m). For mode-I loading, the slope was determined to be m ≈ 7.5,
I
which is more than double the typical value of m ≈ 3 for conventional arc-welded
I
joints.
This steeper slope is interpreted as evidence of a shift in the dominant fatigue
damage mechanism. In conventional welds, fatigue life is primarily governed by
the growth of pre-existing, relatively large welding defects, a process characterized
by a shallow slope (m = 3 − 4). In contrast, the superior quality of the hexapod
specimens (negligible residual stress, as confirmed by the similar performance of
AW and SR specimens, and smaller initial flaws) means that a significant portion
of the fatigue life is spent in the crack initiation or short-crack growth phase. This
initiation-dominated process is characteristic of the base material and results in
a much steeper S-N curve. When analyzed using standard parameters from the
literature, both the S and S models placed the hexapod data in the upper scatter
e T
band of reference data. This provides a safe, conservative life estimate but lacks
accuracy.
However, when dedicated model parameters were derived by fitting directly to the
high-quality hexapod data, the predictive accuracy improved dramatically. The
lifetime standard deviation (σ ) for mode-I data, a measure of scatter, was reduced
N
from∼ 0.39toanexcellent0.21. Thisdemonstratesthataquality-specificresistance
curveisessentialforaccurateassessment. TheS model,withitsfracture-mechanics
T
foundation, consistently yielded slightly better results than the S model, suggesting
e
its formulation is better able to capture the underlying physics. The key parameter
differences are summarized in Table 21.
75

The study provides strong evidence that high-quality welded joints exhibit superior
fatigue resistance characterized not just by a higher fatigue strength (intercept,
log(C)) but, more importantly, by a fundamentally different damage mechanism
(steeper slope, m). This shift is attributed to a prolonged crack initiation phase
resulting from superior manufacturing control, which minimizes residual stresses
and initial defect sizes.
For design engineers, the implications are significant. Standard design codes, which
typically mandate fixed, shallow S-N slopes (e.g., m = 3 or m = 5), are shown to
be highly conservative for welds of verified high quality. While safe, these codes
fail to capture the correct physical behavior and can lead to inefficient designs.
The research advocates for a more nuanced approach to fatigue assessment: where
weld quality can be guaranteed through process control and inspection, the use of
a dedicated, quality-specific resistance curve with a steeper slope is justified. This
allows for more accurate lifetime predictions and opens the possibility for more
optimized, reliable, and lightweight structural designs.
[Sheen et al., 2025] reviewed by K. Hectors
This research addresses a critical structural integrity challenge in modern aero-
engines: predicting the trajectory of fatigue cracks originating from Foreign Object
Damage (FOD) in bladed disks, or ’blisks’. Blisks integrate the blades and disk
into a monolithic component, offering substantial weight and efficiency advantages.
However, this design eliminates the natural crack-arresting features of traditional
dovetail joints, creating a risk that a crack initiated on a blade can propagate di-
rectly into the disk. The trajectory of such a crack is important, as it determines
whether failure occurs via a containable blade-off event or a catastrophic, uncon-
tained disk rupture. The complex stress state in the blade-disk fillet region, aris-
ing from the superposition of high-frequency vibratory bending and low-frequency
rotational stresses, results in non-proportional loading, where the principal stress
directions rotate during an operational cycle. This study investigates the adequacy
of conventional fracture mechanics models in predicting crack paths under these
realistic loading conditions.
To replicate the most important features of the blisk loading environment, a novel
experimental methodology was developed. The study utilized cruciform specimens
machined from Ti-6Al-4V, the primary material for these components, with starter
notches introduced via EDM to simulate FOD sites.
A key innovation was the development of a test rig capable of applying independent
loads in three axes using a standard biaxial machine supplemented with a hydraulic
cylinder. As depicted in Figure 18, this setup applied:
• A radial load (P ), analogous to centrifugal forces.
x
• A high-frequency bending load (P ), representing blade vibration.
y
76

• A hoop load (P ), analogous to the hoop stress in the rotating disk.
z
Two simplified flight block loading configurations were tested. Configuration A
involved high-cycle fatigue in bending (P ) superimposed on static radial and hoop
y
loads, representing a nearly proportional loading condition. Configuration B in-
troduced non-proportionality by cyclically applying the hoop load (P ) once for
z
every 200 cycles of the bending load, mimicking the interaction between engine
start-stop cycles and blade vibration.
Figure 18: Schematic of the three-axis loading (P , P , P ) applied to the cruciform
x y z
specimen.
The experimental campaign successfully generated a wide spectrum of crack trajec-
tories, from nearly straight paths analogous to blade-wise growth (Configuration A)
tohighlydeflectedpathsanalogoustohoop-wisegrowthintothedisk(Configuration
B)
The central finding of the study is the demonstrated inadequacy of the conven-
tional Maximum Tensile Stress (MTS) criterion for predicting crack trajectories
under non-proportional loading. As shown in Figure 19, while the MTS criterion
provided reasonable estimates for the nearly proportional Configuration A tests,
it systematically and significantly underestimated the crack deflection angle for the
non-proportionalConfigurationB tests. The experimentalcracksturnedmuchmore
aggressively under the influence of the cycled hoop load than the model predicted.
Furthermore, post-test fractographic analysis using Scanning Electron Microscopy
(SEM) revealed a dual failure of the conventional Linear Elastic Fracture Mechanics
(LEFM) framework. For Configuration A tests, crack growth rates calculated from
striation spacing correlated well with predictions from a standard Paris/Walker law
model. However, for Configuration B tests, this correlation broke down completely.
The observed growth rates were significantly different from predictions, indicating
77

that the stress intensity factor range, ∆K , is no longer a sufficient parameter to
I
characterize the crack driving force under these complex loading conditions.
Figure 19: Comparison of initial crack trajectory angles observed experimentally versus
predictions from the Maximum Tensile Stress (MTS) criterion for all tests. A significant
discrepancy is evident for Configuration B tests.
In conclusion, this work highlights a critical gap in standard engineering practice
and provides the foundational experimental evidence required to develop the next
generation of fatigue analysis tools needed to ensure the structural integrity of ad-
vanced engineering designs.

The Carpinteri and Spagnoli [2001] model is a physics-based, semi-empirical critical-
plane method for high-cycle multiaxial fatigue of hard (brittle) metals under con-
stant, proportionalornon-proportionalbiaxialnormalplusshearloading(e.g.bend-
ing–torsion in shafts or tubes). Application requires the fully reversed fatigue limits
in bending σ and torsion τ , the S–N slope m for bending, and the complete
af af
multiaxial stress history at the point of interest.
78

The method is validated on three hard metals under synchronous sinusoidal biaxial
√
loading; covers brittle-to-near-ductile ratios (ρ ≈ 1 down to 1/ 3); mean-stress
effects included via plane orientation; phase lag influence found negligible.
Experimental Data Validation is not based on original experimental data. Ex-
perimental results are taken from Nishihara and Kawamoto [1945].

Main conclusions
The paper presents a critical-plane criterion that correlates the plane orientation
with weighted mean principal directions, then assesses failure using a nonlinear
combination of maximum normal stress and shear amplitude on that plane. The
shear vector on the material plane traces an ellipse over the cycle; amplitudes and
means follow from its semi-axes. The approach targets in-phase and out-of-phase
sinusoidal biaxial loading and is compared against Findley/Matake. Validation on
hard metals shows good agreement in the HCF regime.
