The Doudard et al. [2007] model is a probabilistic, physics-based framework for high-
cycle multiaxial fatigue, extending the Weibull weakest-link approach to account for
non-proportionalloadingviaan“activated-slip”volumefactor. Applicationrequires
the macroscopic stress history, the material’s shear modulus µ and Poisson ratio ν,
the Weibull distribution parameters (modulus m, scale S ) of the microscopic yield-
0
stress, and the representative activation volume V .
0
The model is validated on two steels under planar bending, torsion, and combined
histories. The model predicts mean fatigue limits, scatter, and non-proportional
fatigue surfaces with modest mean errors.
Experimental data Themodelseemstobevalidatedonauthors’ownexperimen-
taldataontwosteels(30NiCrMo16andC20), performedoncylindricalsamples, but
the the paper does not give full experimental details (e.g., complete S–N datasets,
exact specimen dimensions, frequencies, full protocols); it mainly reports mechani-
cal properties and mean fatigue limits for the two steels and then uses those values
in the comparison tables. It is not clear if full experimental details and datasets are
included in previous publications.

The paper proposes a probabilistic HCF framework: Poisson activation of micro-
slip sites plus a weakest-link assumption yields a Weibull law that naturally handles
scatter, effective volume, stress heterogeneity, and non-proportional loadings with-
out extra parameters. A directional factor κ(m) accounts for the distribution of
activated slip directions. With only a few parameters, the model matches extensive
multiaxial datasets and provides reliability estimates once the effective volume is
computed.

The Luu et al. [2014] formulation extends classical high-cycle multiaxial fatigue
criteria by embedding stress-gradient terms into both normal and shear compo-
nents, thereby capturing the ”size” (apparent size) and ”gradient” effects (”Smaller
is Stronger” and ”Higher Gradient is Stronger”) observed in smooth, defect-free
metallic specimens under constant-amplitude loading. Application requires the lo-
cal stress history, the material’s fatigue limits under fully-reversed bending and
torsion, and two characteristic lengths/exponents for normal and shear gradient
amplification.
The model is validated on smooth, defect-free steels across: Tension-compression,
bending, torsion, andcombinedloading; Radiifrom 1mmto 30mm; Demonstrated
negligible pure-size effect vs. strong gradient effect (> 10× influence)

The paper extends classical stress-based criteria by inserting explicit gradient terms
in both shear and normal components, with material length scales and exponents
controlling the amplification. The gradient Crossland/Dang Van forms reproduce
“smaller is stronger” and “higher gradient is stronger” while covering a wide range
of loading modes. Identification uses fully reversed torsion (shear-gradient set) and
bending (normal-gradient set), and the model reduces to classical forms when the
| length |         | scales | vanish.  |     |           |     |     |     |     |     |     |

Main conclusions
The paper introduces the stress-scale factor (SSF) surface ssf(σ ,λ) to transform
a
normal-stress damage into the shear scale, defining an equivalent shear stress τ =
eq
max τ +ssfσ . Experiments show that the scale is not constant: it varies with
t a a
stress level and with the stress-amplitude ratio (SAR), i.e., the loading path. Using
the SSF map collapses proportional and non-proportional datasets onto the uniaxial
shear S–N trend with satisfactory accuracy.
1 Original experimental data Anes et al. [2014]
Thisstudymapsatwo–variablestress–scalefactor(ssf)frommultiaxialfatiguetests
on 42CrMo4 steel and uses it to build an equivalent shear stress for life correlation.
Tests were run on smooth hourglass specimens (Figure 20) under stress control at
room temperature, f = 5 Hz. Six loading paths were considered: two uniaxial refer-
ences (axial, shear), three proportional biaxial paths with different stress–amplitude
ratios (SAR), and one non-proportional (90deg out–of–phase) path.
Material and specimens Material: 42CrMo4 (AISI 4140), quenched and tem-
pered. Geometry: smooth hourglass specimen (see Figure 20).
Mechanical and cyclic properties:
Loading paths and levels Five proportional paths with constant SAR and
one non–proportional path (90◦ out–of–phase). The six loading paths and the
stress–amplitude grids used per path are shown in Figure 21

Anes et al. [2017] does not propose a brand-new fatigue criterion, but it refines and
strengthens the same SSF (stress-scale factor) equivalent-shear approach introduced
in 2014 [Anes et al., 2014]. The paper embeds the stress-scale factor damage map
into a critical-plane framework, diagnoses why constant scale factors fail, and pre-
scribes evaluating the equivalent-shear damage at θ = 0◦ (global frame) to avoid
“dual-contribution” bias. Needed inputs: the SSF map ssf(σ,λ) (polynomial coef-
|     | ficients), | and | the | pure-shear | S-N | constants |     | (A,b). |     |     |
| --- | ---------- | --- | --- | ---------- | --- | --------- | --- | ------ | --- | --- |
Experimental data The paper reuses the authors’ earlier 42CrMo4 datasets and
refers the reader to Anes et al. [2014] for experimental procedures.

Method description
1. Critical-plane diagnosis (constant scales fail). Rotate the stress tensor
to an arbitrary plane θ (Mohr) and evaluate Findley’s parameter. The inferred
normal→shear scale kθ is not constant: it varies with θ and with the
Findley
| loading | path | λ.  |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| ------- | ---- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
2. Remove the dual-contribution bias. On generic θ, the local shear mixes
global shear and normal components (two damage scales). The paper pre-
scribes evaluating damage at the global frame θ = 0◦, where this dual contri-
bution vanishes.
3. Apply the SSF map at θ = 0◦. Use the same SSF construction as 2014,
| now | explicitly | in the     | global | frame:    |      |         |        |                     |     |

taking the cycle peak and reading life from the pure-shear S-N. Comparative results
against Findley show an improved collapse across proportional paths, and material

Summary
This paper describes biaxial fatigue tests carried out under a range of loading con-
ditions with cruciform specimens (with a reduced thickness gauge section at the
center) made of Waspaloy. Different biaxiality ratios (expressed as the maximum
strainratioasproposedbyItohetal.) withthesamemaximumprincipalstresswere
92

considered. All tests were load controlled and carried out at a load ratio R=0.05.
The tests were terminated based on the presence of visible cracks.
The experimental results obtained were confronted with three sets of multiaxial
criteria.

93

Summary
Plain and notched axisymmetric specimens have been tested in axial fatigue (R =
−1 and R = 0.1), pure torsion (R = −1), and combined tension and torsion
(R = −1, biaxiality ratio λ = 1 expressed as ratio of nominal torsional shear stress
amplitude to nominal axial stress amplitude). Both in-phase loading and out-of-
phase loading have been explored. Material characterization was complemented by
fractographic analyses carried out on fatigued samples and computed tomography
|     | scan | analyses. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ---- | --------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
The fatigue data were used to calibrate a multiaxial fatigue criterion in combination
with the theory of critical distances (TCD), the line method variant, to account for
the material notch sensitivity. The authors extend an approach to determine the
intrinsic fatigue strength and critical distance length for mode I loading reported
in literature, to torsional loading (mode III) for both the line method and point
method of TCD. The authors average the axial normal stresses and the torsional
shear stresses along the notch bisector over the material’s critical distances, and
not along a path lying on the critical plane. This choice is motivated because of
its easier implementation, empirical evidence, and a systematic comparison of both
|     | approaches |     | in literature. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ---------- | --- | -------------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
With respect to multi-axial fatigue criteria, the paper focuses on critical plane based
approaches: Fatemi-Socie, Modified Wohler Curve method, modification of the
Smith-Watson-Topper normal stress criterion proposed by Chu, and the criterion
proposed by Carpinteri. Because the multiaxial fatigue behavior of the investigated
materialwasfoundtobepredominantlycontrolledbythemaximumprincipalstress,
normal stress-based criteria such as modified Smith-Watson-Topper and the Carpin-
teri et al. criteria were found to yield the most accurate fatigue predictions. The
last criterion works best when combined with a TCD method considering different
|     | TCD       | critical | lengths       | under | mode | I      | and III type | of loading. |     |     |     |     |

A fatigue crack nucleation theory proposed by Tanaka and Mura [1981] was modi-
fied by Wu [Wu, 2017] and further developed for multiaxial loading scenarios [Wu,
2025a]. The model is based on the assumption that dislocation pile-ups lead to
fatigue crack initiation when the energy stored in the material equals the energy re-
quired to form a new crack surface. For the high-cycle fatigue regime, the following
|     | equation | allows | for | a direct | calculation |           |     | of fatigue | life: |       |
| --- | -------- | ------ | --- | -------- | ----------- | --------- | --- | ---------- | ----- | ----- |

|     |          |        |     |          |             | (1−ν)b(∆σ |     | −2σ        | )2    |       |
|     |          |        |     |          |             |           |     | eq         | y     |       |
where w is the surface free energy, b is the Burgers vector, and R is a coefficient
s s
related to surface roughness. The author states that R = 1 for a smooth surface,
s
and R = 1/3 for a machined or ground surface. ∆σ denotes the range of the
|     | s           |     |            |     |         |     |     |     | eq  |     |
| --- | ----------- | --- | ---------- | --- | ------- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     | Huber–Mises |     | equivalent |     | stress. |     |     |     |     |     |
It should be noted that the surface free energy is not a material parameter that can
be easily determined experimentally, and few literature sources report its values for
engineering alloys. In [Wu, 2025b], the model was subsequently extended to account
for the mean stress effect. The model has not been validated for random loading
cases; however, this issue was identified as an important direction for future studies
|     | [Wu, 2025a]. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |

| [Tomaszewski |     | and | Tomasz, |     | 2025] | reviewed |     | by  | T. Tezel |     |
| ------------ | --- | --- | ------- | --- | ----- | -------- | --- | --- | -------- | --- |
ThestudybyTomaszewski(2025)investigatestheHighCycleFatigue(HCF)strength
scatter of 316L stainless steel produced via Selective Laser Melting (SLM). The
investigation targets failure lives exceeding 105 cycles where the material nomi-
nally remains within the elastic range. A probabilistic link is established between
manufacturing-induced defects and the fatigue limit distribution. The experimen-
tal validation in this study is limited to uniaxial fully reversed loading conditions
(R = −1). Multiaxiality is introduced through the use of the Crossland stress in-
variant rather than through multiaxial loading paths. No cycle-counting procedure
is employed, as the proposed framework focuses on fatigue strength distribution and
95

orientations (adapted from Tomaszewski (2025))
Figure 25: Summary of static mechanical properties for specimens built in horizontal and
vertical directions (adapted from Tomaszewski (2025))
scatter at a fixed number of cycles rather than cumulative damage under variable
loading.Experimental results are provided in the form of processed data, includ-
ing P–S–N curves and Weibull distribution parameters. Raw stress–time histories
or load spectra are not reported. This work represents a probabilistic multiax-
ial HCF approach based on stress invariants. Its main contribution lies in linking
manufacturing-induced defect populations to fatigue strength scatter rather than in
modeling complex loading paths.
The proposed model utilizes a semi-empirical approach based on the Crossland
multiaxial criterion. The normalized equivalent stress is expressed as:

The article by Tveit et al. (2025) presents an invariant-based fatigue damage frame-
workforHighCycleFatigue(HCF).Theproposedmodelisformulatedwithinacon-
tinuum mechanics framework and is based on stress tensor invariants (I , J , J ).
1 2 3
Fatigue damage is treated as a time-continuous internal variable.The formulation
does not rely on any cycle-counting technique. Fatigue damage evolves continu-
ously in time, making the model theoretically applicable to arbitrary stress histo-
ries.Although the framework is theoretically applicable to non-proportional multi-
axial loading, experimental validation presented in the article is primarily restricted
to proportional loading conditions. This study provides a physics-based, cycle-
counting-free reference framework for multiaxial HCF and serves as a benchmark
|     | for | continuum |     | damage | mechanics |     | approaches. |     |     |
| --- | --- | --------- | --- | ------ | --------- | --- | ----------- | --- | --- |
Developedwithinthecontinuous-timeframeworkofOttosenetal. (2008), themodel
treats fatigue damage (D) as a time-continuous evolution variable. The core of the
|     | framework |     | is  | the damage | evolution |         | law, expressed | as:         |       |
| --- | --------- | --- | --- | ---------- | --------- | ------- | -------------- | ----------- | ----- |
|     |           |     |     |            | ˙         |         | ˙              | ˙           |       |
|     |           |     |     |            | D         | = g(f)f | for f >        | 0 and f > 0 | (150) |
where f is the endurance function. This formulation eliminates the need for dis-
crete cycle-counting principles, such as Rainflow, and makes the model theoretically
|     | applicable |     | to  | arbitrary | multiaxial |     | stress histories. |     |     |
| --- | ---------- | --- | --- | --------- | ---------- | --- | ----------------- | --- | --- |
The endurance condition is defined through an invariant-based function f(σ), where
its zero-level set represents the endurance surface. The model utilizes the first in-
variantofthestresstensor(I ), andthesecondandthirdinvariantsofthedeviatoric
1
stress tensor (J ,J ). The inclusion of J via the Lode angle allows the endurance
|     |     |     |     | 2 3 |     |     | 3   |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
surface to exhibit a star-convex 60-degree symmetry in the deviatoric plane, as
|     | illustrated |     | in  | Figure | 1.  |     |     |     |     |
| --- | ----------- | --- | --- | ------ | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

The article by Wang et al. (2022) proposes a new multiaxial high-cycle fatigue
failure criterion and life prediction model based on the critical plane method. The
study focuses on the High Cycle Fatigue (HCF) domain (N > 105 cycles) where the
f
material deformation is nominally elastic. The proposed methodology is a stress-
based critical plane approach formulated for constant amplitude cycling under both
|     | proportional |     | and | non-proportional |     |     | loading | conditions. |     |     |     |
| --- | ------------ | --- | --- | ---------------- | --- | --- | ------- | ----------- | --- | --- | --- |

effectusingamodifiedSmith-Watson-Topper(SWT)parameterdefinedontheplane
of maximum shear stress. The governing equivalent stress equation is defined as:

The article by Zhang et al. (2024) presents a multiscale-based multiaxial fatigue
model for Short Fiber Reinforced Polymer (SFRP) composites, specifically PP-
GF30. The study is strictly situated in the High Cycle Fatigue (HCF) domain
(N > 105 cycles) where the material nominally remains within the elastic range.
f
The proposed methodology is a physics-based approach utilizing Representative
Volume Elements (RVE) to determine the local stress state at the fiber-matrix
interface.
The core innovation is the focus on the local interface stress rather than macroscopic
stress invariants. The fatigue damage is governed by the local normal and shear
|     | stress | components |     | acting | on  | the fiber | interface. |     |
| --- | ------ | ---------- | --- | ------ | --- | --------- | ---------- | --- |
The equivalent stress range (∆σ ) is formulated based on the local interface stress
eq

(cid:112)

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | ---- | --------- | ----- |
|     |     |     |     |     |     | eq   | n         |       |
where ∆σ and ∆τ are the local normal and shear stress ranges at the interface,
n
|     | and | η is | a scaling | factor | for | shear sensitivity. |     |     |

Validationresults, asillustratedinFigure37, showthatthemodelcollapsesdifferent
multiaxial states into a single master curve, accounting for the microstructural local
stresses. 84.4% of the predictions fall within the 10x error band.
The main conclusion is that the local interface stress range is the primary driver
of HCF damage in SFRPs, allowing for accurate life prediction of arbitrary fiber
orientations.

| [Merot |          | et al., | 2024]      | reviewed  |     | by  | V. Toven |            |          |     |
The study by M´erot et al. (2024) introduces a novel non-proportionality factor,
f , based on the Convex Hull (CH) of the loading path in the stress or strain
CH
deviatoricspace. Theresearchaimstoovercomethelimitationsoftraditionalfactors
(like those based on the Minimum Circumscribed Enclosure) by providing a more
robust geometric description of the loading path. The investigation is focused on
High Cycle Fatigue (HCF) and validated using the extensive FatLim database.
The model and associated experimental data are classified as follows:
• Variable space: Stress or strain-based (applicable to both deviatoric spaces).
|     |     | • Type: |     | Semi-empirical |     | /   | Geometric. |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | ------- | --- | -------------- | --- | --- | ---------- | --- | --- | --- |
•
Aspect: Specifically targets non-proportional (out-of-phase) loading paths.
•
|     |     | Cycling: |     | Constant |     | amplitude | (periodic) |     | loading paths. |     |
| --- | --- | -------- | --- | -------- | --- | --------- | ---------- | --- | -------------- | --- |
The core contribution is the definition of the f factor, which measures the ”full-
CH
ness” of the loading path by comparing the area of its convex hull to the area of its
|     | Minimum |     | Circumscribed |     |     | Circle | (MCC):      |     |       |       |
| --- | ------- | --- | ------------- | --- | --- | ------ | ----------- | --- | ----- | ----- |
|     |         |     |               |     |     |        | Area(Convex |     | Hull) |       |
|     |         |     |               |     |     | f      | =           |     |       | (162) |
CH
Area(MCC)
This factor is integrated into fatigue criteria (such as the modified Liu & Zen-
ner model) to account for the additional hardening or damage caused by non-
proportionality.
The authors conclude that: (i) the f factor provides a more accurate and stable
CH
representation of non-proportionality compared to traditional phase-difference or
integral-based factors; (ii) it effectively distinguishes between different path shapes
(e.g., circular vs. cruciform); and (iii) when applied to the FatLim dataset, it
significantly improves the life prediction accuracy of stress-based criteria.

The study by ZhiQiang et al. (2021) proposes a comprehensive multiaxial fatigue
damageparameterthatadaptstodifferentmaterialbehaviorsandloadingconditions
within the High Cycle Fatigue (HCF) regime. The model is built upon the critical
plane approach, where the selection of the critical plane and the formulation of the
damage parameter are governed by the material’s ductility and the loading path
characteristics.

The model was validated using experimental data from several metallic materials,
including 30CrNiMo8, 1045 steel, and Al7075-T6, under both proportional and non-
proportional loading paths. The authors conclude that the proposed damage pa-
rameter successfully captures the transition between shear-dominated and normal-
stress-dominated fatigue mechanisms, providing high life-prediction accuracy across
|     | different |     | HCF | regimes. |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |     |
